The code assignment follows the instructions. One value per trial, so repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n392\t\n393\t## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *tongue_y_position* derived from?\n394\t\n395\ti. From `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, whose `data` is `(n_frames, 3)` = `tongue_x`, `tongue_y`, `tongue_likelihood`, with matching `timestamps`. Column 1 (`tongue_y`) is the value; column 2 (`likelihood`) decides whether the tongue is visible in that frame.\n396\t\n397\tii.\n398\t```python\n399\tts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\n400\tt_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\n401\tdata = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\n402\ty = data[:, 1].copy()\n403\t```\n404\t\n405\tiii. This is the only tongue measurement in the file. The channel layout is stated in the series' own `description` attribute (`\"('tongue_x', 'tongue_y', 'tongue_likelihood')\"`) rather than assumed. Tracking is present in all 174 sessions and runs at ~294 Hz.\n406\t\n407\t## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_y_position*?\n408\t\n409\ti. Four steps. Frames with `likelihood < 0.5` are set to NaN, since the tracker still reports a position when the tongue is not protruding. The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. Bins containing no visible frame are assigned a fourth class, `3` (`'not visible'`).\n410\t\n411\tii.\n412\t```python\n413\tTONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\n414\tTONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\n415\tTONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n416\t```\n417\t\n418\t```python\n419\ty[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n420\t\n421\t# bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\n422\tgidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n423\tedges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n424\t\n425\t# per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\n426\tcls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\n427\tlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n428\thi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n429\tfor i in range(len(go)):\n430\t    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n431\t    np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n432\t    m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n433\t    ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n434\t    cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n435\t```\n436\t\n437\tiii. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. The likelihood value is effectively binary (89% of frames below 0.01, 10.5% at or above 0.99), so the exact threshold does not matter.\n438\t\n439\tPercentiles are taken over the 50 ms bin means rather than raw frames so the edges are defined on the same quantity that gets discretised; taking them over frames instead skews the resulting class balance, because averaging within a bin pulls values toward the center. The 40th/60th split and the per-session scope both follow the instructions. A fourth class is defined for bins with no visible tongue, which is 75% of all bins.\n440\t\n441\t## 8-d. How is `output` *tongue_y_position* aligned with the neural data?\n442\t\n443\ti. This is the one genuinely time-varying output, so it is the only one needing real alignment. The camera timestamps are on the same session-absolute clock as the spikes and the go cues, so each trial's frame range is found by `searchsorted` on the camera timestamps at `go + T_START` and `go + T_STOP`, and frames are assigned to bins by their offset from `go + T_START` — the same go-cue-relative grid used for the firing rates.\n444\t\n445\tii.\n446\t```python\n447\tlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n448\thi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n449\tfor i in range(len(go)):\n450\t    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n451\t```\n452\t\n453\tiii. The camera timestamps share the global clock with the spikes and events, so no interpolation or offset correction is needed — the same bin grid is applied to both streams, which guarantees bin `k` of the tongue output covers the same interval as bin `k` of the firing rates.\n454\t\n455\tUnlike the spikes, the video is trial-gated: it runs from `trials.start_time` to the trial-end event and is off during the inter-trial interval. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n456\t\n457\t## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n458\t\n459\ti. Three cases:\n460\t\n461\t- **Session never quality-controlled**: `classification` and `anno_name` are NaN rather than strings. `_text` maps any non-string entry to `''`, the session then has no `'good'` units, and it is dropped.\n462\t- **Trials without spike data**: excluded via `obs_intervals` and `free_water` (see 1-e).\n463\t- **Frames with no tracked tongue**: set to NaN and excluded from the bin mean; a bin left with no valid frame becomes the `'not visible'` class.\n464\t\n465\tii.\n466\t```python\n467\tdef _text(col):\n468\t    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n469\t    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n470\t```\n471\t\n472\t```python\n473\tgood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n474\tif good.size == 0:\n475\t    return None\n476\t```\n477\t\n478\t```python\n479\ty[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n480\t...\n481\tm = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n482\tok = ~np.isnan(m)\n483\tcls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n484\t```\n485\t\n486\tiii. Missing data is handled one of two ways depending on what it means. Where nothing was recorded, the session or trial is excluded, since emitting it would fabricate data — a trial with no spikes would otherwise appear as 4 s of 0 Hz across every unit. Where the measurement legitimately has no value, as with a retracted tongue, it is represented as an explicit category rather than imputed.\n487\t\n488\t## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n489\t\n490\ti. Reading each NWB file dominates. The full conversion takes 247 s for 174 sessions, ~1.4 s per session on average, ranging from ~0.7 s to ~4 s roughly with unit count. Within a session the costs are pulling the `spike_times` buffer (up to ~11.5 M doubles) and the tongue tracking array (~680 k x 3), then the per-unit `searchsorted` loop. Pickling the 11.9 GB result takes a further ~40 s.\n491\t\n492\tii. N/A\n493\t\n494\tiii. The work is dominated by I/O and by one binary search per bin edge per unit, both of which scale with the data actually needed. No step was worth optimising further given the 15-minute budget in the instructions.\n495\t\n496\t## 10-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n497\t\n498\ti. Two loops remain. The per-unit loop in the neural binning runs one `searchsorted` per unit, but over *all* trials at once — the per-trial dimension is already vectorised by flattening the edge array. The per-trial loop in `tongue_output` bins one trial's frames at a time.\n499\t\n500\tii.\n501\t```python\n502\tedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n503\tfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n504\t    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n505\t    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n506\t```\n507\t\n508\tiii. The tongue loop could be vectorised with a global bin index and one `bincount`, but it runs over trials rather than frames and is not a measurable share of runtime, so it was left in the clearer form.\n509\t\n510\t## 10-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n511\t\n512\ti. Nothing is recomputed. Each NWB file is opened once and every quantity derived from it is computed once. The bin grid (`REL_EDGES`, `CENTERS`) is built once at module level and reused for every trial and session.\n513\t\n514\tii. N/A\n515\t\n516\tiii. The conversion is a single pass over the files. N/A\n525\t\n526\t## 10-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n527\t\n528\ti. Firing rates are stored as `float32` and the outputs as `int8` rather than the default `float64`. Each session is read inside a `with` block so the file handle and its cached arrays are released on exit, and only the per-trial slices are retained. The full neural payload is 11.9 GB; a single session's working arrays are at most ~0.2 GB.\n529\t\n530\tii.\n531\t```python\n532\trates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n533\t```\n534\t\n535\t```python\n536\tout = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n537\t```\n538\t\n539\t```python\n540\tinp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n541\t```\n542\t\n543\tiii. `float32` halves the largest object in the output relative to `float64` at no cost, since firing rates are multiples of 20 Hz and well within its precision. Outputs are small non-negative integers, so `int8` is sufficient. Peak memory is set by the accumulated result rather than by any single session, so no further streaming was needed.\n544\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"8e513440-8ad1-483e-9bdc-345ed49c50e0","uuid":"95f32916-fd29-43a4-b804-759f9db1a4a3","timestamp":"2026-09-23T20:27:19.726Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/tests/reference_DECISIONS.md","content":"# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once. Subjects, trials, and units are then read from within each file (`nwb.subject`, `nwb.trials`, `nwb.units`, `nwb.acquisition`).\n\nii. Finding all data:\n```python\nfiles = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n...\nfor i, path in enumerate(files, 1):\n    res = process_session(path)\n```\n\nLoading one session:\n```python\nwith NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n    nwb = io.read()\n    units = nwb.units\n    trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n```\n\niii. NWB is the published format for this dataset and `pynwb` is its standard reader. Since there is one file per session, the directory listing is the complete set of sessions and a glob is sufficient; sorting it makes the session order deterministic. The resulting counts (174 files, 28 subjects) match `assetsSummary` in `data/dandiset.yaml`.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Each NWB file records its animal in `nwb.subject.subject_id`, a numeric string such as `'440956'`. That value is read for every session and carried through to assembly, where `subjects` is the sorted set of unique ids and `subject_idx` gives each session's index into that list.\n\nii. Per session:\n```python\nreturn {\n    'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n    ...\n```\n\nAt assembly:\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n...\n'subjects': subjects,\n'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n```\n\niii. `subject_id` is the canonical animal identifier in the file, and the containing folder name (`sub-440956`) is derived from it, so no separate grouping step is needed. This gives 28 subjects with 3-10 sessions each, matching the dandiset. The numeric id differs from the mouse name used in the papers — `nwb.identifier` is e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, so subject `440956` is mouse `SC015` — but the numeric id is what the NWB subject field provides, so it is used directly.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. One NWB file is one session, so no grouping or splitting is needed. Each session is identified by `nwb.identifier` (e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, encoding mouse, date, time and session number), recorded per session and also listed in `metadata['session_info']`. Session order in the output follows the sorted file list.\n\nii.\n```python\nfiles = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n```\n\n```python\n'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n```\n\n```python\n'session_info': [\n    {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n     'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n    for r in results\n],\n```\n\niii. The dandiset already stores one session per file, so the file boundary is the session boundary and nothing has to be inferred. Because the filename embeds the acquisition timestamp (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_...`), sorting the paths puts sessions in chronological order within each subject. 173 of the 174 files reach the output; the one exception is dropped for having no quality-controlled units (see 2-c).\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Trials come from the NWB trials table (`nwb.trials`), one row per behavioural trial, with exactly one go-cue event per row. The row count is checked against the number of go-cue events.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\ngo = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\nassert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n```\n\niii. Trials are clearly defined by the trials table, so it is used directly rather than re-deriving boundaries from the event streams. `go_start_times` has exactly one event per trial in all 174 sessions, which makes the mapping unambiguous. This is not true of the other trial-phase events: `sample_start_times` and `delay_start_times` can have several entries per trial, because a lick during the sample or delay replays that epoch.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered only for absence of spike data, in two cases. First, trials outside `units/obs_intervals`: in 8 sessions the ephys recording starts after the behaviour, leaving up to 376 leading trials with no spikes. Second, `free_water` trials, which have no spikes anywhere in the window even though `obs_intervals` covers them. A session is dropped entirely if fewer than 2 trials survive. No behavioural quality filter is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\n# filter behavioral trials with no spike data\noi_off = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].data)\noi_start = np.concatenate([[0], oi_off[:-1]])\nobs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].target.data)[oi_start[good[0]]:oi_off[good[0]]]\nkeep = np.isin(np.round(trials['start_time'].values, 4), np.round(obs[:, 0], 4))\nassert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n\n# filter no water trials\nkeep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\nif keep.sum() < 2:\n    return None\ntrials = trials[keep]\ngo = go[keep]\n```\n\niii. `obs_intervals` is the file's own record of which trials were observed, and its start times match `trials.start_time`, which makes the mapping exact. The `free_water` exclusion was found empirically — those trials are covered by `obs_intervals` yet contain no spikes at all — and accounts for 2,449 of the 2,451 all-zero trials remaining after the `obs_intervals` filter.\n\nEarly-lick and no-response (`ignore`) trials are deliberately kept, even though the data paper states \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis\", because both are required decoder outputs here. The 2-trial minimum is the target format's requirement.\n\nTogether these remove 3,510 of the 94,370 trials in the retained sessions (3.7%), leaving 90,860.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times`, the sorted spike times of each unit in session-absolute seconds. Only units with `classification == 'good'` contribute (see 2-c). The go-cue times (`BehavioralEvents/go_start_times`) are the other input, used to place the bin edges.\n\nii.\n```python\noffs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\nallst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\nstarts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n```\n\niii. `spike_times` is the only neural representation in the file, so firing rates are computed from it directly.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are converted to per-bin firing rates in Hz. For each good unit, the bin edges for every trial are built as one flat array of absolute times, `np.searchsorted` gives the running spike count at each edge, and differencing adjacent counts gives the spike count per bin. Counts are divided by the bin width to give Hz. No smoothing, normalisation, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\nrates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n```\n\niii. Firing rate is computed from the binned spike count over the 50 ms window defined in the instructions. This also matches the reference code, which calls `sliding_histogram(..., rate=True)` and returns `binSpikes / bin_width`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `units/classification == 'good'` are kept. No thresholds are applied to any individual quality metric. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. This retains 69,453 of 272,227 units (25.5%), a median of 390 per session.\n\nii.\n```python\n# ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\ncls = _text(units['classification'])\ngood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\nif good.size == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. `classification` is the verdict of the spike-sorting quality-control classifier described in `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`.\n\n`units/unit_quality` ('good' / 'multi') is deliberately not used: it is an older label that disagrees on 12.2% of the classifier-good units and is far more permissive.\n\nThe one dropped session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) has `classification` and `anno_name` set to NaN for all 1,852 units, i.e. it was never quality-controlled. Excluding it gives 173 sessions and 69,453 good units, against the white paper's 173 sessions and 69,943 units.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Spike times and event times are already on the same session-absolute clock, so no alignment step is needed. The bin edges relative to the go cue are added to each trial's go-cue time to give the absolute time window for that trial, and the spikes are binned against those edges directly.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n```\n\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n```\n\niii. Everything in the NWB file is timestamped on one global clock, so aligning to the go cue only requires looking up each trial's go-cue time and taking the window around it. There is no resampling or interpolation, and no per-stream offset to correct.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once, as 81 edges relative to the go cue, and reused for every trial and session, so every trial has the same 80 timepoints.\n\nii.\n```python\n# trial window relative to the go cue, and bin width, in seconds\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n```\n\niii. The window and the 50 ms bin width are set by the instructions. Defining the grid once as offsets from the go cue gives a constant 80 timepoints per trial, which the target format requires.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* derived from?\n\ni. From `sample_start_times`, the tone onsets of the session, together with the go cue of each trial. The tone taken for a trial is the **last one** before its go cue.\n\nii. The tone of each trial:\n```python\nsample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\ntone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n```\n\niii. An early lick replays the sample epoch, so a trial can carry more than one tone; the last one before the go cue is the one the animal actually used.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_tone_onset*?\n\ni. The bins sit around the go cue, so the value of a bin is its center plus the gap between the tone and the go cue.\n\nii. The bin center, and the shift that turns them into time from the tone:\n```python\nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n```python\n# bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\ntime_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n```\n\niii. No additional processing needed other than find the tone onset time.\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is how the neural binnning grid is defined.\n\nii. The grid, defined once as offsets from the go cue, and laid on each trial's go cue to bin the spikes:\n```python\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *photostim* derived from?\n\ni. From `photostim_onset` and `photostim_duration` in the trials table, with `start_time` and the go cue used to place them on the trial's time axis.\n\nii. The onset and offset of the stimulation, relative to the go cue:\n```python\non_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\nhas = on_s != 'N/A'\nstim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\nstim_off[has] = stim_on[has] + trials['photostim_duration'].values[has].astype(float)\n```\n\niii. The onsets are stored as strings measured from trial start, with `'N/A'` on the trials that were not stimulated, so they have to be converted and re-expressed against the go cue the bins are aligned on.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *photostim*?\n\ni. A bin is 1 where its center falls between the onset and the offset of the stimulation and 0 elsewhere, so the input is a binary time series rather than a per trial flag.\n\nii. The bins the light is on for:\n```python\n# NaN compares False, so non-stim trials stay 0\nphotostim = ((CENTERS[None, :] >= stim_on[:, None])\n             & (CENTERS[None, :] < stim_off[:, None]))\n```\n\niii. A trial without stimulation keeps NaN bounds, and NaN compares false, so all of its bins come out 0 without a separate branch.\n\n## 4-c. How is `input` *photostim* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The onset and offset are expressed relative to the go cue, which is the event the neural bins are aligned on, so they can be compared against the bin center directly.\n\nii. The onset put on the same axis as the bins:\n```python\nstim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *choice* derived from?\n\ni. There is no choice column in the file. Choice is derived from two trials-table columns: `trial_instruction` (`'left'` / `'right'`, the side the tone instructed) and `outcome` (`'hit'` / `'miss'` / `'ignore'`). A hit means the animal licked the instructed side, a miss means it licked the other side, and an `ignore` means it never licked.\n\nii.\n```python\nSIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\nCHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n```\n\n```python\n# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\noutcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\nside = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\nchoice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n```\n\niii. The animal's actual lick direction is not stored, but it is fully determined by the instructed side and the outcome, so it is derived from those two columns. `outcome == 'ignore'` was verified to mean no lick anywhere in `[go, go + 1.5]`, with no exceptions, so choice is genuinely undefined on those trials and gets its own third value.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *choice*?\n\ni. The derived choice is coded as `0` left, `1` right, `2` no lick, and written into row 0 of the per-trial output array, repeated across all 80 bins. `output_values[0]` names the three codes.\n\nii.\n```python\nSIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\nCHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n\nOUTPUT_VALUES = [\n    ['left', 'right', 'no lick'],\n    ...\n]\n```\n\n```python\n# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\noutcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\nside = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\nchoice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n\n# per-trial values are repeated across bins so all outputs share one array\nout = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\nout[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\n```\n\niii. `left = 0` and `right = 1` follow the instructions, and a third class is defined for the no-lick case. Choice is one value per trial, so it is repeated across the 80 bins to keep all four outputs in a single `(n_output, n_timepoints)` array.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Outcome comes directly from the `outcome` column of the trials table, which already holds the strings `'ignore'`, `'miss'`, and `'hit'`.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n```\n\niii. The trials table stores the outcome explicitly with exactly the three categories the instructions ask for, so no derivation is needed.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *outcome*?\n\ni. The three strings are mapped to `0` ignore, `1` miss, `2` hit via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 1 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nOUTCOME_CODE = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\n```\n\n```python\nout[:, 1, :] = np.array([OUTCOME_CODE[x] for x in outcome_s])[:, None]\n```\n\niii. The code assignment follows the instructions. Outcome is one value per trial, so it is repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *early_lick* derived from?\n\ni. From the `early_lick` column of the trials table, which holds the strings `'no early'` and `'early'`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials['early_lick'].values\n```\n\niii. The trials table flags early licking explicitly, so no derivation is needed. The lick that sets the flag occurs during the sample or delay epoch, before the go cue, so the event itself falls inside the -2.5 s window even though the flag is stored per trial.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *early_lick*?\n\ni. The two strings are mapped to `0` no, `1` yes via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 2 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nEARLY_CODE = {'no early': 0, 'early': 1}\n```\n\n```python\nout[:, 2, :] = np.array([EARLY_CODE[x] for x in trials['early_lick'].values])[:, None]\n```\n\niii. The code assignment follows the instructions. One value per trial, so repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *tongue_y_position* derived from?\n\ni. From `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, whose `data` is `(n_frames, 3)` = `tongue_x`, `tongue_y`, `tongue_likelihood`, with matching `timestamps`. Column 1 (`tongue_y`) is the value; column 2 (`likelihood`) decides whether the tongue is visible in that frame.\n\nii.\n```python\nts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\nt_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\ndata = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\ny = data[:, 1].copy()\n```\n\niii. This is the only tongue measurement in the file. The channel layout is stated in the series' own `description` attribute (`\"('tongue_x', 'tongue_y', 'tongue_likelihood')\"`) rather than assumed. Tracking is present in all 174 sessions and runs at ~294 Hz.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_y_position*?\n\ni. Four steps. Frames with `likelihood < 0.5` are set to NaN, since the tracker still reports a position when the tongue is not protruding. The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. Bins containing no visible frame are assigned a fourth class, `3` (`'not visible'`).\n\nii.\n```python\nTONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\nTONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\nTONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n```\n\n```python\ny[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n\n# bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\ngidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\nedges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n\n# per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\ncls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\nlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\nhi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\nfor i in range(len(go)):\n    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n    np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n    m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n    ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n    cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n```\n\niii. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. The likelihood value is effectively binary (89% of frames below 0.01, 10.5% at or above 0.99), so the exact threshold does not matter.\n\nPercentiles are taken over the 50 ms bin means rather than raw frames so the edges are defined on the same quantity that gets discretised; taking them over frames instead skews the resulting class balance, because averaging within a bin pulls values toward the center. The 40th/60th split and the per-session scope both follow the instructions. A fourth class is defined for bins with no visible tongue, which is 75% of all bins.\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *tongue_y_position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. This is the one genuinely time-varying output, so it is the only one needing real alignment. The camera timestamps are on the same session-absolute clock as the spikes and the go cues, so each trial's frame range is found by `searchsorted` on the camera timestamps at `go + T_START` and `go + T_STOP`, and frames are assigned to bins by their offset from `go + T_START` — the same go-cue-relative grid used for the firing rates.\n\nii.\n```python\nlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\nhi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\nfor i in range(len(go)):\n    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The camera timestamps share the global clock with the spikes and events, so no interpolation or offset correction is needed — the same bin grid is applied to both streams, which guarantees bin `k` of the tongue output covers the same interval as bin `k` of the firing rates.\n\nUnlike the spikes, the video is trial-gated: it runs from `trials.start_time` to the trial-end event and is off during the inter-trial interval. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Three cases:\n\n- **Session never quality-controlled**: `classification` and `anno_name` are NaN rather than strings. `_text` maps any non-string entry to `''`, the session then has no `'good'` units, and it is dropped.\n- **Trials without spike data**: excluded via `obs_intervals` and `free_water` (see 1-e).\n- **Frames with no tracked tongue**: set to NaN and excluded from the bin mean; a bin left with no valid frame becomes the `'not visible'` class.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef _text(col):\n    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n```\n\n```python\ngood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\nif good.size == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\n```python\ny[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n...\nm = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\nok = ~np.isnan(m)\ncls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n```\n\niii. Missing data is handled one of two ways depending on what it means. Where nothing was recorded, the session or trial is excluded, since emitting it would fabricate data — a trial with no spikes would otherwise appear as 4 s of 0 Hz across every unit. Where the measurement legitimately has no value, as with a retracted tongue, it is represented as an explicit category rather than imputed.\n\n## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading each NWB file dominates. The full conversion takes 247 s for 174 sessions, ~1.4 s per session on average, ranging from ~0.7 s to ~4 s roughly with unit count. Within a session the costs are pulling the `spike_times` buffer (up to ~11.5 M doubles) and the tongue tracking array (~680 k x 3), then the per-unit `searchsorted` loop. Pickling the 11.9 GB result takes a further ~40 s.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The work is dominated by I/O and by one binary search per bin edge per unit, both of which scale with the data actually needed. No step was worth optimising further given the 15-minute budget in the instructions.\n\n## 10-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Two loops remain. The per-unit loop in the neural binning runs one `searchsorted` per unit, but over *all* trials at once — the per-trial dimension is already vectorised by flattening the edge array. The per-trial loop in `tongue_output` bins one trial's frames at a time.\n\nii.\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n```\n\niii. The per-unit loop cannot be collapsed further because each unit has a different number of spikes, so there is no single sorted array to search — this is inherent to the ragged storage. The tongue loop could be vectorised with a global bin index and one `bincount`, but it runs over trials rather than frames and is not a measurable share of runtime, so it was left in the clearer form.\n\n## 10-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. Nothing is recomputed. Each NWB file is opened once and every quantity derived from it is computed once. The bin grid (`REL_EDGES`, `CENTERS`) is built once at module level and reused for every trial and session.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The conversion is a single pass over the files. Because the tongue discretisation edges are per-session rather than global, they can be computed inside that same pass — no second pass over the data is needed to establish them.\n\n## 10-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. None. Every field computed goes into the output.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 10-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n\ni. Firing rates are stored as `float32` and the outputs as `int8` rather than the default `float64`. Each session is read inside a `with` block so the file handle and its cached arrays are released on exit, and only the per-trial slices are retained. The full neural payload is 11.9 GB; a single session's working arrays are at most ~0.2 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n```\n\n```python\nout = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n```\n\n```python\ninp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. `float32` halves the largest object in the output relative to `float64` at no cost, since firing rates are multiples of 20 Hz and well within its precision. Outputs are small non-negative integers, so `int8` is sufficient. Peak memory is set by the accumulated result rather than by any single session, so no further streaming was needed.\n","numLines":544,"startLine":1,"totalLines":544}}}

differencing gives the count per bin\n            pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n            rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n        rates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n\n        return {\n            'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n            'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n            'neural': [np.ascontiguousarray(rates[:, t, :]) for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'input': [inp[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'output': [out[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'regions': regions,\n            'n_trials': n_trials,\n            'n_units': int(good.size),\n        }\n\n\ndef assemble(results):\n    \"\"\"Stitch per-session results into the target dictionary.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n\n    brain_regions = sorted({g for r in results for g in r['regions']})\n    reg_ix = {g: i for i, g in enumerate(brain_regions)}\n\n    return {\n        'neural': [r['neural'] for r in results],\n        'input': [r['input'] for r in results],\n        'output': [r['output'] for r in results],\n\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n\n        'brain_regions': brain_regions,\n        'brain_region_idx': [np.array([reg_ix[g] for g in r['regions']], np.int32)\n                             for r in results],\n\n        'input_names': INPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_names': OUTPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_values': OUTPUT_VALUES,\n\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description':\n                'Auditory delayed-response task. A 3 kHz or 12 kHz tone (0.65 s '\n                'sample epoch) instructs a left or right lick, followed by a 1.2 s '\n                'delay; a 6 kHz go cue opens a 1.5 s response window. Decoded variables: '\n                'lick direction, outcome, early lick, tongue y-position.',\n            'time_bin_size': BIN * 1000.0,          # ms\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'go cue onset (auditory, 6 kHz, 0.1 s)',\n            'off_start': T_START,\n            'off_end': T_STOP,\n\n            'dataset': 'DANDI 000363, Mesoscale Activity Map (Chen et al. 2023)',\n            'neural_units': 'Hz',\n            'unit_curation':\n                \"units/classification == 'good', the QC classifier of Chen, \"\n                'Colonell, Li & Svoboda (2023). n_trials_dropped_outside_obs = 0\n247\t\n248\t    for trial_idx in range(len(trial_start)):\n249\t        start = trial_start[trial_idx]\n250\t        stop = trial_stop[trial_idx]\n251\t        go_candidates = interval_values(go_times, start, stop)\n252\t        if len(go_candidates) == 0:\n253\t            raise ValueError(f\"{path.name}: no go cue found for trial {trial_idx}\")\n254\t        go_time = float(go_candidates[-1])\n255\t        window_start = go_time + WINDOW_START_S\n256\t        window_end = go_time + WINDOW_END_S\n257\t        covered = np.any(\n258\t            (window_start >= session_obs_intervals[:, 0])\n259\t            & (window_end <= session_obs_intervals[:, 1])\n260\t        )\n261\t        if not covered:\n262\t            n_trials_dropped_outside_obs += 1\n263\t            continue\n264\t\n265\t        sample_candidates = interval_values(sample_start_times, start, go_time)\n266\t        if len(sample_candidates) == 0:\n267\t            sample_onset = go_time - 1.85\n268\t            n_missing_sample_onset_fallback += 1\n269\t        else:\n270\t            sample_onset = float(sample_candidates[-1])\n271\t\n272\t        bin_centers_abs = go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL\n273\t        time_from_tone = (bin_centers_abs - sample_onset).astype(np.float32)\n274\t\n275\t        photostim_row = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n276\t        stim_onset = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_onset[trial_idx])\n277\t        stim_dur = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_duration[trial_idx])\n278\t        stim_power = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_power[trial_idx])\n279\t        if stim_power is not None and stim_onset is not None and stim_dur is not None:\n280\t            stim_start_abs = start + stim_onset\n281\t            stim_stop_abs = stim_start_abs + stim_dur\n282\t            photostim_row = (\n283\t                (bin_centers_abs >= stim_start_abs) & (bin_centers_abs < stim_stop_abs)\n284\t            ).astype(np.float32)\n285\t\n286\t        choice_code, choice_source = infer_choice(\n287\t            instruction=trial_instruction[trial_idx],\n288\t            outcome=trial_outcome[trial_idx],\n289\t            trial_start=start,\n290\t            trial_stop=stop,\n291\t            left_lick_times=left_lick_times,\n292\t            right_lick_times=right_lick_times,\n293\t        )\n294\t        choice_sources[choice_source] += 1\n295\t        outcome_code = OUTCOME_MAP[trial_outcome[trial_idx]]\n296\t        early_code = EARLY_MAP[trial_early[trial_idx]]\n297\t\n298\t        tongue_frame_idx = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, bin_centers_abs, side=\"right\") - 1\n299\t        tongue_frame_idx = np.clip(tongue_frame_idx, 0, len(processed_tongue_y) - 1)\n300\t        tongue_y = processed_tongue_y[tongue_frame_idx]\n301\t        tongue_bin = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.int16)\n302\t        tongue_bin[tongue_y >= tongue_info[\"p40\"]] = 1\n303\t        tongue_bin[tongue_y > tongue_info[\"p60\"]] = 2\n304\t\n305\t        output_row = np.empty((4, N_BINS), dtype=np.int16)\n306\t        output_row[0, :] = choice_code\n307\t        output_row[1, :] = outcome_code\n308\t        output_row[2, :] = early_code\n309\t        output_row[3, :] = tongue_bin\n310\t        trial_records.append(\n311\t            {\n312\t                \"raw_trial_idx\": trial_idx,\n313\t                \"go_time\": go_time,\n314\t                \"sample_onset\": sample_onset,\n315\t                \"time_from_tone\": time_from_tone,\n316\t                \"photostim_on\": photostim_row,\n317\t                \"output\": output_row,\n318\t            }\n319\t        )\n320\t\n321\t    n_trials = len(trial_records)\n322\t    if n_trials == 0:\n323\t        raise ValueError(f\"{path.name}: all trials were excluded by observation-interval coverage.\")\n324\t\n325\t    trial_edge_matrix = np.empty((n_trials, N_BINS + 1), dtype=np.float64)\n326\t    input_tensor = np.empty((n_trials, 2, N_BINS), dtype=np.float32)\n327\t    output_tensor = np.empty((n_trials, 4, N_BINS), dtype=np.int16)\n328\t    go_per_trial = np.empty(n_trials, dtype=np.float64)\n329\t    sample_on_per_trial = np.empty(n_trials, dtype=np.float64)\n330\t    for trial_edge_matrix[keep_idx] = rec[\"go_time\"] + BIN_EDGES_REL\n334\t        input_tensor[keep_idx, 0, :] = rec[\"time_from_tone\"]\n335\t        input_tensor[keep_idx, 1, :] = neural_tensor[:, unit_idx, :] = (counts / BIN_WIDTH_S).astype(np.float16)\n343\t\n344\t    nonzero_trial_mask = np.any(neural_tensor != 0, axis=(1, 2))\n345\t    n_trials_dropped_all_zero = int((~nonzero_trial_mask).sum())\n346\t    if n_trials_dropped_all_zero:\n347\t        neural_tensor = neural_tensor[nonzero_trial_mask]\n348\t        input_tensor = sample_on_per_trial[nonzero_trial_mask]\n352\t        trial_records = [rec for rec, keep in zip(trial_records, nonzero_trial_mask, strict=True) if keep]\n353\t        n_trials = len(trial_records)\n354\t\n355\t    neural_trials = [neural_tensor[i] for i in range(n_trials)]\n356\t    input_trials = [input_tensor[i] for i in range(n_trials)]\n357\t    output_trials = [output_tensor[i] for i in range(n_trials)]\n358\t\n359\t    plot_payload = None\n360\t    if make_plot:\n361\t        # Pick a representative non-early, non-ignore trial when possible.\n362\t        candidate = np.where(\n363\t            (output_tensor[:, 1, 0] != OUTCOME_MAP[\"ignore\"])\n364\t            & (output_tensor[:, 2, 0] == EARLY_MAP[\"no early\"])\n365\t        )[0]\n366\t        trial_idx = int(candidate[0] if len(candidate) else 0)\n367\t        raw_trial_idx = int(trial_records[trial_idx][\"raw_trial_idx\"])\n368\t        plot_payload = {\n369\t            \"trial_idx\":       stats=stats,\n415\t        plot_payload=plot_payload,\n416\t    )\n417\t\n418\t\n419\tdef plot_processing(result: SessionResult, out_path: Path) -> None:\n420\t    payload = {}\n510\t    brain_regions: list[str] = []\n511\t    region_to_idx: dict[str, int] = {}\n512\t\n513\t    neural = []\n514\t    decoder_input = []\n515\t    output = []\n516\t    subject_idx = epoch: three 150 ms tones with 100 ms inter-tone intervals; total sample epoch shown as `0.65 s`\n125\t  - delay epoch: `1.2 s`\n126\t  - go cue: `0.1 s`\n127\t  - response epoch / answer period: `1.5 s`\n128\t  - consumption period: `1.5 s`\n129\t  - inter-trial interval: `250 ms`\n130\t- Reference analyses are aligned to go cue for neural/video preprocessing and for many figures (`time to go` axis; code explicitly aligns lick/stim/marker data to go cue).\n131\t- Method-paper neural preprocessing: 40 ms firing-rate bins with 3.4 ms stride.\n132\t- Video tracking:\n133\t  - side-view video only for method-paper analyses.\n134\t  - marker/video timestamps at 300 Hz.\n135\t  - tongue/jaw/nose markers tracked; method paper additionally mentions paws, but the local NWB side-view tracking stream clearly contains tongue/jaw/nose.\n136\t- Video preprocessing from method paper:\n137\t  - marker outliers removed using a five-sigma velocity threshold and imputed from nearby frames.\n138\t  - when the tongue is occluded in the mouth, tongue position is set to its mean value.\n139\t- Photoinhibition:\n140\t  - late delay epoch only, final 0.5 s including 100 ms ramp-down.\n141\t  - photoinhibition ends before the go cue.\n142\t\n143\t### Curation Steps\n144\t\n145\t**Neuron curation rules**:\n146\t- Spike sorting with Kilosort2, followed by 15 quality metrics.\n147\t- Recordings with substantial drift were rejected at the penetration level (`ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`, p. 1).\n148\t- Region-specific logistic-regression QC classifiers were trained for five major brain divisions: cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain, medulla.\n149\t- Region-to-classifier mapping from `methods.txt` / white paper:\n150\t  - cortex classifier: ALM, other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory, cortical subplate\n151\t  - striatum classifier: striatum, pallidum\n152\t  - thalamus classifier: thalamus, hypothalamus\n153\t  - midbrain classifier: midbrain, pons\n154\t  - medulla classifier: medulla, cerebellum\n155\t- White paper reports classifier performance AUC `> 0.9` on average and final false-alarm rates of `7.8%` cortex, `6.4%` striatum, `7.3%` thalamus, `5.5%` midbrain, `4.3%` medulla (`methods.txt`).\n156\t\n157\t**Trial curation rules**:\n158\t- Early lick trials and no-response / ignore trials were excluded for many paper analyses.\n159\t- Overall behavioral performance was defined on control trials only, excluding early licks.\n160\t- Sessions selected for analysis required:\n161\t  - performance `> 65%`\n162\t  - at least `50` correct lick-left and `50` correct lick-right control trials\n163\t- Method-paper reporting summary adds another analysis-level exclusion:\n164\t  - brain areas with fewer than `10` neurons per session were excluded from analysis (`methodpaper.pdf`, p. 27).\n165\t- Specific decoder analyses in papers used further task-dependent restrictions:\n166\t  - population choice decoder used balanced trial subsampling of `50 LL`, `50 RR`, `10 LR`, `10 RL` trials\n167\t  - some video-choice analyses excluded sessions with fewer than `20` trials in any of four groups (`methodpaper.pdf`, p. 14)\n168\t\n169\t### Decoders COMPLETE\n181\t\n182\t### Discrepancies Found\n183\t| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n184\t|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n185\t| Good-unit definition | `code/VideoAnalysisUtils/preprocessing_DJ_2022Aug.py` uses external classifier-derived “good units” lists from QC `.mat` files | NWB embeds both `units.unit_quality` and `units.classification` | White paper says logistic-regression classifiers label units as `good` vs `unlabelled` and those lists are used for analysis | Use `units.classification == \"good\"` as the local equivalent of the external QC lists. `unit_quality == \"good\"` is too permissive and produces 154,948 units, which is far above the published total. |\n186\t| Session count | Reference code processes sessions with saved QC-filtered neurons | Raw data contain 174 NWB files | Papers repeatedly state `173 behavioral sessions` | Exclude the single session `sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508_behavior+ecephys+ogen.nwb`, which has `classification == nan` for all 1,852 units and zero classifier-labeled good units. This reconciles the session count to 173. |\n187\t| Good-unit total after resolving session scope | External QC lists expected to approximate published counts | Local NWB `classification == \"good\"` yields 69,453 good units across the 173 sessions with at least one good unit | Papers state 69,943 good units | Treat the remaining 490-unit gap (0.7%) as a likely archive/export-version discrepancy and trust the embedded NWB classifier labels for conversion. The qualitative match is very close, while all alternative local interpretations are much worse. |\n188\t| Behavioral session inclusion | Reference code for video/r2 analyses often uses custom trial masks rather than whole-session exclusion | Applying `>65%` performance and `>=50` correct control trials per lick direction leaves only 152/174 sessions | `methods.txt` states these criteria for “selected experimental sessions for analysis” | Treat these as analysis-specific selection criteria for paper figures, not as the base dataset definition. For conversion, do not drop to 152 sessions by default; use the broader 173-session QC-filtered dataset unless a later sanity check suggests otherwise. |\n189\t| Marker set | `align_markers.py` expects `nose`, `tongue`, `jaw`, `whisker`; method paper text mentions `jaw`, `paws`, `tongue` | Local NWB side-view tracking streams contain `TongueTracking`, `JawTracking`, `NoseTracking` | Papers only require tracked orofacial movements and use side-view video | For this task, use the directly available side-view tongue trajectory from NWB. No paw/whisker reconstruction is needed because the target output only requires tongue y-position. |\n190\t| Regional labeling | Preprocessing code groups neurons into 14 major region classes and uses histology annotations for area membership | Electrode metadata give bilateral target regions; `units.anno_name` provides finer anatomical labels, often empty for non-localized units | Papers report both major compartments and finer subregional analyses | Use classifier-good units and derive coarse region labels from electrode target metadata for base `brain_regions`, while keeping finer `anno_name` available for sanity checks if needed. |\n191\t| Alignment / binning | Reference code and papers use go-cue alignment, 40 ms bins, 3.4 ms stride | Raw NWB stores absolute trial/event timestamps and continuous video at 3.4 ms resolution | User task requires go-cue alignment with 50 ms bins from `-2.5 s` to `+1.5 s` | Preserve the reference alignment/QC logic but intentionally deviate in final bin width/window to satisfy the decoder task. This is an explicit task-driven modification, not a misunderstanding of the reference pipeline. |\n192\t\n193\t---\n194\t\n195\t## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n196\t**Status**: COMPLETE\n197\t\n198\t### Variable Mapping\n199\t| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n200\t|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n201\t| `units.spike_times` for units with `classification == \"good\"` | `neural` | For each session/trial, bin absolute spike times into go-aligned `[-2.5, 1.5)` windows using 50 ms bins; convert counts to firing rates (`count / 0.05`) | `sliding_histogram`, `process_one_area` | Task-driven deviation from reference 40 ms bins; keep go-cue alignment and QC logic the same. |\n202\t| `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times.timestamps` | alignment event / metadata | One go cue per trial, found by matching event timestamp into `[trial.start_time, trial.stop_time]` | reference code aligns all streams to go cue | Use event-in-trial matching rather than row order assumptions. |\n203\t| `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times.timestamps` | `input[0]` (`time_from_tone_onset_s`) | For each trial, use the **last** sample-start event before the go cue as the effective tone/sample onset; store `(bin_center_time - sample_onset_time)` in seconds for every bin | code aligns task epochs to go cue; methods describe replays after early licks | Early-lick trials can contain multiple sample-start events; the last one is the final replay that leads to the observed go cue. |\n204\t| `trials.photostim_onset`, `trials.photostim_duration`, `trials.start_time` | `input[1]` (`photostim_on`) | Convert trial-relative onset/duration to absolute time, then to go-aligned binary series over bins | reference preprocessing shifts stimulation times relative to go cue | Trials with `photostim_power == N/A` are all zeros. |\n205\t| `trials.trial_instruction` + `trials.outcome` | `output[0]` (`choice`) | For `hit`/`miss`, infer actual lick choice from instruction and outcome: hit -> instructed side, miss -> opposite side | paper/code derive LL/RR/LR/RL from instruction + correctness | Verified against first post-go lick on sampled files: 0 mismatches in 10,084 responded trials. |\n206\t| first lick in trial, else instruction fallback for ignore trials | `output[0]` (`choice`) | If `outcome == ignore`, choice is undefined in source. Planned fallback: use earliest lick side anywhere in the trial if present; otherwise use instructed side and document this as an unavoidable placeholder | no direct reference equivalent because paper excludes ignore trials | This is the main task-specific edge case to revisit if decoder accuracy is poor. |\n207\t| `trials.outcome` | `output[1]` (`outcome`) | Map strings to integers: `ignore=0`, `miss=1`, `hit=2`; replicate across all time bins | direct from raw trial table | Matches user specification exactly. |\n208\t| `trials.early_lick` | `output[2]` (`early_lick`) | Map `no early=0`, `early=1`; replicate across all time bins | direct from raw trial table | Matches user specification exactly. |\n209\t| `BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking` `(y, likelihood)` | `output[3]` (`tongue_y_bin`) | Process continuous tongue y per session, resample to trial/bin centers, then discretize with session-level 40th and 60th percentiles into classes `0/1/2` | methods text describes tongue outlier handling and mean imputation when occluded | Planned preprocessing: 5-sigma velocity outlier detection + interpolation; low-likelihood/occluded frames imputed to session mean tongue y. |\n210\t| `units.anno_name` | `brain_region_idx` / `brain_regions` | Use exact non-empty anatomical annotation string as neuron region label | papers use histology-based anatomical assignments | All classifier-good units checked so far have non-empty `anno_name`; this avoids mislabeling orbital/other-cortex neurons as coarse probe target labels. |\n211\t| `subject.subject_id` | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Store unique subject IDs and per-session indices | direct from NWB subject metadata | Session order follows sorted NWB file list after excluding the zero-good-unit session. |\n212\t\n213\t### Key Decisions\n214\t1. **Base session set**: Use the 173 NWB sessions with at least one classifier-labeled good unit. This matches the paper’s session count better than the raw 174-file archive or the much stricter 152-session behavioral subset.\n215\t2. **Unit filter**: Use `units.classification == \"good\"` rather than `units.unit_quality == \"good\"`. This is the only local field consistent with the classifier-based QC described in the white paper and methods.\n216\t3. **Neural representation**: Store firing rates, not spike counts, because the task explicitly asks for 50 ms bins “for computing firing rates”.\n217\t4. **Tone onset handling**: For early-lick replay trials, define tone onset as the last sample-start event before go. This matches the actual final sample epoch that temporally precedes the measured go cue.\n218\t5. **Output shape**: Make outputs 2D time-varying arrays `(4, 80)` for every trial. Choice/outcome/early-lick will be repeated across bins; tongue y will vary over time. This satisfies the decoder format cleanly and makes the time-varying output explicit.\n219\t6. **Choice edge case on ignore trials**: Because the source does not contain an explicit choice label when no response occurs, use the earliest lick side in the trial if available; otherwise fall back to instructed side. This is a task-driven compromise and will be stress-tested with decoder performance and sanity checks.\n220\t7. **Brain-region metadata**: Use exact `anno_name` strings instead of coarse insertion targets. This preserves histology-derived localization and avoids systematically mislabeling orbital and other cortical units as ALM.\n221\t8. **Video resampling**: For each 50 ms neural bin, assign tongue y from the processed continuous tongue trace at the bin center (or closest preceding frame). This is closer to the reference marker-alignment logic than averaging over long windows.\n222\t9. **Reference-consistent deviation**: Keep reference curation and go-cue temporal alignment, but intentionally change only the final bin width/window to `50 ms` and `[-2.5, +1.5] s` because the decoder task requires it.\n223\t\n224\t### Planned Sanity Checks\n225\t- [ ] Neural check: for selected session/trial/unit tuples, recompute raw spike counts from NWB `units.spike_times` and go timestamp, convert to Hz, and compare to converted `neural` bins with `np.allclose()`.\n226\t- [ ] Input check: for selected trials, recompute sample onset and photostim interval directly from NWB events/tables and compare to converted `time_from_tone_onset_s` and `photostim_on` arrays with `np.allclose()`.\n227\t- [ ] Output check: for selected hit/miss trials, verify converted `choice`, `outcome`, and `early_lick` against raw trial table / lick events with `np.allclose()`.\n228\t- [ ] Tongue check: for selected session/trial/bin combinations, compare converted tongue class against raw processed tongue y and the session percentiles used for discretization.\n229\t- [ ] Dataset-size
